"[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:4000]\n# K-FW-012：CPU 低位执行：解包、补偿与累加路径\n\n低位权重首先改变存储表示。是否同时减少计算时间，取决于实际执行的是低位乘加、解包后的整数乘加，还是反量化后的浮点乘加。CPU 上还受线程组织、cache、NUMA、指令选择和实际 dispatch 影响；不能从文件位宽直接推出端到端收益。本章从冻结量化制品出发，追踪码、参数、局部和与输出单位怎样实现同一次线性层。\n\n## 问题、对象与完成条件\n\n设输出通道数为 N、归约维为 K、token 数为 M。采用 W[N,K]、X[K,M]、Y[N,M]，定义 Y[n,m]=Σ_k W[n,k]X[k,m]。这与 CPU GEMM/GEMV 章节采用的 Y=XW 是轴约定不同；衔接时需要转置逻辑视图，不能直接沿用数组下标。加载器改变权重轴序时，scale、zero point、组身份和打包布局必须同步映射。\n\n输入对象包括固定制品的 packed codes P、冻结尺度 S、整数零点 Z 和布局版本；本调用的实数激活 X，或共同产生并冻结的 QX、SX、ZX；以及执行计划的 tile、线程划分、乘法与累加精度、epilogue 和实际内核路径。输出是声明 dtype 下的 Y 或 QY。诊断时可另保存解码值、逻辑组号、有效范围、整数部分和与内核身份，避免仅凭最终 shape 判断正确性。\n\n状态链为 FROZEN → TILE_DECODED → ACC_PARTIAL → EPILOGUE → PUBLISHED。FROZEN 表示码和参数只读；TILE_DECODED 中的 q 仍是整数码，未必已经物化实数权重；ACC_PARTIAL 可以是整数点积、激活单位的组内和或实数输出部分和，三者不能混用；EPILOGUE 只处理完成归约的输出；PUBLISHED 才允许消费者使用完整结果。失败时丢弃未发布部分，取消后仍须等待在途访问和最后读者结束，才能复用 scratch。\n\n正确性不变量是：每个有效权重位置有唯一逻辑组；参数来自对应冻结表；每个有效乘积恰好贡献一次；无效填充不进入归约；不同尺度的部分和先恢复共同单位再相加；每个输出只执行一次 epilogue。N、K、M 可以为零。K=0 时实数归约结果为零，非空输出仍须加 bias 并转换；N=0 或 M=0 时没有输出元素，合法空集合不提供非空数值证据。\n\n## 三类执行选择与合法条件\n\n| 路径 | 数据流 | 可能节省的资源 | 必需条件与新增成本 |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| 权重量化、实数激活，例如 W4A16 | 解包 q，恢复权重后 FMA，或采用组补偿式 | 权重存储与候选读取字节 | 组轴、符号和保存后的参数正确；增加移位、转换、参数读取或组和 |\n| 权重与激活都量化，例如 W8A8 | 整数点积、零点补偿、逐组缩放 | 权重和激活字节，可能利用整数乘加 | 激活码与参数同调用冻结；检查指令 signedness、累加宽度和输出单位 |\n| 敏感区保留较高精度 | 部分层或通道走高精度，再合并 | 降低表示近似的质量风险 | 分支与合并保持函数接口；增加布局、驻留、切换与同步 |\n\nW4A16 中的 A16 是路径名称，不能代替完整数值契约。激活可以以 FP16/BF16 存储，但乘法、局部和、最终输出累加与转换精度仍须分别声明。\n\n完整展开 FP32 权重与 tile 内解包是缓存策略。前者可能复用成熟浮点内核，但另占完整高精度副本；若每轮写出再读取，新增交通可能抵消压缩收益。后者缩短高精度对象生存期，却可能因 M 分块或 cache 驱逐而重复解包。选择取决于 M、重用距离、线程、cache 与准备摊销，不能只按位宽决定。\n\n## 仿射补偿、分组与输出单位\n\n对一个参数恒定的有效 K 段，令 w_k=s_w(q_wk−z_w)、x_k=s_x(q_xk−z_x)，段长为 L，则\n\nΣ_k w_k x_k = s_w s_x · {Σ_k q_wk q_xk − z_w Σ_k q_xk − z_x Σ_k q_wk + L z_w z_x}。\n\nL 是该段实际消费的元素数，不是无条件的完整组长，也不是补齐后的 tile 宽度。不同组的 s_w s_x 不同时，不能先合并所有整数和，再乘一个全局尺度。对称权重 z_w=0 消去相关项，却不自动消去 z_x 的补偿。\n\nW4A16 的 X 已是实数，采用\n\nΣ_k w_k X[k,m] = s_w · {Σ_k q_wk X[k,m] − z_w Σ_k X[k,m]}。\n\nq_w X 与 X 的组和仍保留激活的数值单位，乘 s_w 后才成为输出贡献。z_w=0 时可省去第二组和，尺度仍须按权重组使用。两条恒等式在精确算术下成立；浮点实现改变求和顺序后不保证逐位相同。\n\n设 q_x=(5,7)、z_x=5、s_x=0.5；q_w=(2,0)、z_w=1、s_w=2。解码向量为 (0,1) 与 (2,−2)，点积为 −2。整数展开为 10−12−10+10=−2，尺度乘积为 1。这个例子同时检查补偿符号、零点和真实零的身份。\n\n权重与激活可以沿 K 使用不同组长 G_w、G_x。在当前位置 a，权重组号为 floor(a/G_w)，激活组号为 floor(a/G_x)。下一段终点取权重组末、激活组末、当前 k tile 末和 K 的最小值，使段内两套参数均恒定。共同细化段有自己的长度，但参数仍分别索引原权重表和原激活表，不能把共同段编号当作两张表的共同组号。\n\nbias 也必须带单位。若 b_real=s_b b_int，只有整数 accumulator 的单位恰为 s_b，才能直接加入 b_int。逐 K 组尺度不同或激活尺度随 token 改变时，一个整数 bias 通常没有覆盖所有组和 token 的共同倍率；本章算法在实数 epilogue 中加 bias。输出量化时再计算 q_y=clip(R(y/s_y)+z_y,q_min,q_max)，其中 y 已完成 bias 与声明的激活。残差支路也须先统一单位。\n\n## 固定格式：INT4 分组码流\n\n本章具体格式为 INT4-ROW-LOWFIRST-AFFINE-v1，只支持 b=4。逻辑权重为 N×K，signed INT4 码域为 [−8,7]。每行从新字节开始，行跨度 H_bytes=ceil(K/2)；偶数 k 使用低 nibble，奇数 k 使用高 nibble。码地址为 n H_bytes+floor(k/2)，位移为 4(k mod 2)。nibble 8…15 分别解释为 −8…−1。\n\n权重组沿 K 划分，组号 g=floor(k/G)，G 为正整数。H_groups=ceil(K/G)，S、Z 的形状均为 N×H_groups。S 使用制品实际保存后的有限正值；Z 是 [−8,7] 中的整数。K 为奇数时最后一个高 nibble 固定为 0，属于规范化的无效填充，不属于任何有效元素，也不保证解码为实数零；消费者必须按 K 屏蔽它。另一种允许消费填充的格式必须另行提供对应参数和数学中性码，不能借用本格式的无效位置。\n\n准入检查包括非负整数 shape、正整数 G、位宽和版本、码流精确长度、参数表形状、有限正尺度、合法整数零点及奇尾规范值。K=0 时码流和组表为空。编码输入还须有限；NaN、Inf、scale 下溢为零或溢出应触发拒绝，或进入明确定义的高精度例外路径。\n\n```pseudocode\n算法 PACK4-FROZEN\n输入：有限 W[N,K]，已冻结 S、Z，正整数 G，布局版本 v\n输出：packed P、shape、有效长度与 v，或拒绝\n\n1 校验 N,K、G、b=4 和 v\n   H_groups=ceil(K/G)；H_bytes=ceil(K/2)\n   校验 S/Z 形状为 N×H_groups，S 有限且正，Z 为 [-8,7] 内整数\n2 分配 N*H_bytes 字节并清零；此后 S、Z 只读\n3 对 n=0..N-1、k=0..K-1：\n     g=floor(k/G)\n     q=clip(floor(W[n,k]/S[n,g]+1/2)+Z[n,g],-8,7)\n     u=q mod 16；h=n*H_bytes+floor(k/2)\n     若 k 偶数：将 P[h] 的低 nibble 写为 u，保留高 nibble\n     否则：将 P[h] 的高 nibble 写为 u，保留低 nibble\n4 奇数 K 的行尾高 nibble 保持 0，并记录有效长度 K\n5 返回 P 与原样冻结参数的身份；不重新拟合或再次舍入尺度\n```\n\n```pseudocode\n算法 UNPACK4-VALID\n输入：已完成格式准入的 P、N,K、v，以及有效位置 n,k\n输出：signed q，或拒绝\n\n1 只接受 0<=n<N、0<=k<K\n2 h=n*ceil(K/2)+floor(k/2)\n   u=(P[h] >> (4*(k mod 2))) & 15\n3 q=u 若 u<8，否则 q=u-16\n4 返回 q；调用者按 (n,floor(k/G)) 查询冻结参数\n```\n\n舍入规则固定为 R(t)=floor(t+1/2)，即最近整数、半整数向正无穷。它不同于某些宿主语言的默认最近偶数舍入。编码前先转换并冻结 scale 的存储精度，再用实际保存值编码；解码和补偿也使用同一值。导出时改变 scale，却沿用旧码和旧补偿结论，会改变制品含义\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 8000:14625]\n对值之和不超过\n\nL·{A_w A_x+|z_w|A_x+|z_x|A_w+|z_w z_x|}。\n\n该界可用于选择补偿宽度；最终中心化结果较小不能证明展开中间式安全。对完整 signed INT8 码域、零点也在该域、L≤131071 的段，该界至多 131071×65536=8,589,869,056，需要比 int32 更宽的类型，signed int64 可容纳。更窄向量中间和仍须另证局部累加次数与指令饱和行为。bias 若在整数域加入，还要检查初值与单位。\n\n完整 signed INT8 码域与零初值下，长度 131072 的整段 int32 计划拒绝；允许分段时可切为 131071 与 1，在更宽类型中合并得到 2,147,483,648。若合并后重新写回 int32，分段并未解决问题。排除 −128 的合同可能接受更长归约，但必须连同编码和入口检查一起成立。\n\n舍入发生在参数存储、编码、局部浮点运算、定点倍率与输出转换等不同位置。负半整数 tie 规则、饱和及非有限值处理必须逐处定义。本章编码所选半整数向正无穷规则不代表所有输出定点指令的规则。整数码一致性、浮点容差一致性和任务质量是不同结论，不能用最终文本偶然相同代替中间检查。\n\n## CPU 映射、线程与缓冲生存期\n\n固定行布局使同一输出通道的 K 码连续。对连续 X[K,M]，激活地址为 base_X+e_X(kM+m)，e_X 是激活元素字节数。一个可重建的外积组织是：线程取得独占的 [n0,n1)×[m0,m1) 输出区；从 n H_bytes+floor(k/2) 提取 q 并广播；向量 lane l 对应 m=m0+l，加载相邻激活，更新自己的组和与输出 accumulator。m≥m1 的 lane 不读取输入、不写输出。W8A8 同样使用各 n 的 qw 广播与各 m 的 qx 向量，保持各输出的独立索引。\n\nM=1 时可以沿 K 向量化。对交段 [a,e)，lane l 在第 t 步对应 k=a+tV+l；只在 k<e 时读取码与激活，否则向局部和贡献零，不读取行尾填充。各 lane 保存点积或组和；段结束后按固定配对树合并，先相邻两项，再每四项、每八项，直到完成向量归约。补偿和缩放仍使用该段参数，不能跨不同尺度段合并原始整数和。沿 K 的选择增加水平归约并改变浮点顺序；沿 M 的选择则增加同一码对多个 token 的复用。\n\n这是逻辑元素到地址、lane、局部和及写回的组织说明，不指定某个 CPU 的寄存器数量或私有指令映射。目标 ISA、编译目标、操作系统状态、指令 signedness、饱和和窄中间累加必须与合同相容。例如把 signed 激活改成 q'_x=q_x+128 时，零点也须改成 z'_x=z_x+128，并以新的 [0,255] 码域重新检查原始点积及补偿范围，不能只改加载类型。\n\n沿 N 或 M 分线程通常能让每个输出只有一个写者，省去最终归约；相邻元素仍可能共享 cache line，引起 false sharing。M=1 时沿 M 没有足够并行度；N 也小时可考虑 split-K。各线程先产生实数部分和，合并后只执行一次 bias 与 epilogue。P_t 个线程的部分和约需 P_t BN BM e_acc 字节，并增加读写、归约与完成同步。要求可复现时固定合并树。\n\n每线程可独占激活 panel 和 scratch。若共享解包 tile，先由唯一写者生成并发布，全部消费者结束后才允许下一 tile 覆盖。预取和双缓冲增加同时驻留对象；加载完成不等于读取结束。失败或取消不解除已经提交的访问责任。\n\n只读权重可按 NUMA 域复制以减少远端访问，但每个物理副本都增加容量和准备。共享一份节省容量，却可能使多个域重复远端读取；通信账应包括远端链路流量、跨域激活与 split-K 部分和，不能只数文件字节。具体选择需结合线程亲和、页归属、cache 与服务并发取证。\n\n## 条件化成本：组与 tile 使用同一口径\n\n逐行 INT4 布局的容量为\n\nB_code=N ceil(K/2)，B_meta=N ceil(K/G)m，B_w=B_code+B_meta+B_align。\n\nm 是每组 scale、zero point 和所计对齐字段的实际字节数。逐行格式不能套用整张量连续位流的 ceil(NK/2)：N=K=3 时逐行为 6 字节，连续位流才是 5 字节。有效位宽为 8B_w/(NK)，只对 NK>0 定义。\n\n对 4096×4096、G=128、m=4，码为 8,388,608 字节，元数据为 524,288 字节，合计 8,912,896 字节，即 8.5 MiB、4.25 bit/weight，未计额外对齐、版本、长度表、重打包副本与工作区。同形状 FP32 为 64 MiB；完整展开另需这份驻留，若每轮写出再读回，至少新增 128 MiB 有效载荷交通。\n\n补偿工作由实际交段数决定。令 T=ceil(K/BK)、H=ceil(K/G)，J 为所有 k tile 与逻辑量化组的非空交段数。K=0 时 J=0。对从零开始的均匀分块，K>0 时\n\nJ=1+floor((K−1)/BK)+floor((K−1)/G)−floor((K−1)/lcm(BK,G))。\n\n这是内部 tile 边界与组边界取并集后的区间数，满足 max(T,H)≤J≤min(K,T+H−1)。分割互相嵌套时 J=max(T,H)；只有 tile 不额外切开组时，J 才等于 H。K=128、G=128、BK=1 时逻辑组只有一个，当前算法却执行 128 次补偿、尺度应用与输出更新。\n\n因此 W4A16 当前算法的核心乘加为 O(MNK)，交段补偿、缩放和实数输出更新为 O(MNJ)。这些更新可以在寄存器或 cache 中完成，不能直接视为 MNJ 次 DRAM 写。xsum 在所写循环中随每个 n 重算，其加法也进入 O(MNK)；将它按激活段共享可以减少重复计算，但需增加共享状态、发布与复用证明。\n\n令 R_M=ceil(M/BM)、R_N=ceil(N/BN)。所写 M 分块组织中，每个权重可能解包 R_M 遍，解包工作为 O(R_M NK)，参数读取请求可能达到 O(R_M NJ)。激活 panel 也可能由不同 n tile 重读；cache 命中改变下层流量。奇数 k tile 边界还可能让相邻 tile 读取同一字节的不同 nibble，进一步区分有效元素消费与字节请求。\n\nW8A8 的 J_8 使用 k tile、权重组、激活组及算术安全分段边界的共同细化，补偿与尺度应用为 O(MN J_8)。所写 sum_qw、sum_qx 的重复工作分别约为 O(R_M NK)、O(R_N MK)。固定权重整组的 Σq_w 可以预计算，但当前交段若只覆盖部分组，不能直接拿整组和替代；应增加交段表、前缀和或改变分段方案，并计准备和容量。\n\n另一种算法可以跨 tile 保留同组部分和，直到组结束再缩放一次，将相应更新次数降到 H；代价是更长的状态生存期、组身份、可能的跨线程合并及浮点顺序变化。不能在当前逐交段算法上直接使用该成本。\n\n若一次前向只读取每个压缩权重和元数据一次，忽略激活与输出，权重口径算术强度约为\n\nI_w=2MNK/B_w≈2M/(b/8+m/G)。\n\nb=4、G=128、m=4 时，M=1 为 64/17≈3.76 FLOP/字节，M=32 为 2048/17≈120.5。这不是完整设备算术强度；实际流量须加入重复读取因子 R_w、边界字节、cache 与 NUMA 路径。设定可持续带宽为 80 GiB/s，仅一次 8.5 MiB 权重读取下界约 0.104 ms，64 MiB 为 0.781 ms，均不是端到端预测。\n\n单线程 tile 驻留至少包括实数输出 BN×BM、激活 BK×BM、组内和及地址状态。W4A16 可以另保留 BN×BK 解包码或高精度权重；W8A8 还需 BN×BM 整数 I、BN+BM 的码和及更宽补偿临时量。寄存器不足造成 spill 后，原先假设的近端复用会变成额外访存。每线程副本、双缓冲、NUMA 副本和完整高精度展开须进入同一时刻的峰值账，共享物理存储只计一次。\n\n准备包括校准、量化、参数存储转换、打包、布局重排、内核选择与预热。一次准备为 C_prep，每次调用节省 ΔT、实际复用 R 次时，只有 RΔT>C_prep 才可能在所计窗口内收回；ΔT≤0 时增加复用不会扭转。动态激活量化是每次调用的统计、编码、读写和发布成本，不能放入只付一次的权重准备。\n\n同步包括动态尺度归约、共享 panel 发布、split-K 合并与最终完成；通信包括远端读取和跨域消息。关键路径按依赖与共享资源计算：可重叠资源需求先给必要下界，再加确实暴露的串行等待，不能把全部工作量直接相加，也不能假定完全重叠。容量、请求字节、设备事务与时间分别报告。\n\n## 条件变化后的保留、调整与放弃\n\n| 变化 | 保留的关系 | 调整对象与新增成本 | 放弃或改选依据 |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| M 很小 | 压缩减少候选权重字节 | 比较解包、参数访问、水平归约与 dispatch | 解包超过读取节省时选较高精度或 W8 |\n| M 增大 | 一个权重可服务更多 token | 增加 M 方向复用，重算 accumulator、激活和 R_M | 低位路径计算效率不足时选成熟矩阵内核 |\n| G 或 BK 变小 | 逻辑码仍由原组解释 | 分别重算 H、J、元数据和更新次数 | 交段补偿或参数流量成为主项时改块或布局 |\n| G_w 与 G_x 不同 | 仿射点积恒等式保留 | 共同细化，保留两套原组索引 | 把共同段号直接索引原表的计划应拒绝 |\n| scale 存储精度改变 | 码由保存后的参数解释 | 重新冻结、编码并检查重建值 | 尺度不再有限正值时拒绝 |\n| 位宽或 signedness 改变 | 可重新定义量化数学 | 更换完整布局和真实码域，重算准入与补偿 | 沿用 nibble 或旧零点规则时拒绝 |\n| z_w=0 | 权重仿射式保留 | W4A16 可省 xsum，W8A8 仍检查 z_x | 未证明激活对称时不能继续删补偿 |\n| K 增大或初值非零 | 每段消费和单位不变量保留 | 重算全部中间界，必要时分段或扩大类型 | 无合法宽度计划时拒绝 |\n| 线程、并发或 NUMA 改变 | 输出身份和最后使用关系保留 | 重测私有 cache 重读、远端流量与同步 | 争用超过并行收益时调整预算与放置 |\n| 敏感层恢复高精度 | 模型接口与消费者一致 | 增加分支、驻留与合并成本 | 质量或 SLO 仍不达标时放弃组合 |\n| 校准分布漂移 | 旧制品仍可按旧参数解码 | 重采校准，检查饱和与任务质量 | 不达质量要求时提高精度或停止该近似 |\n\n这些是条件化选择。CPU 与 GPU 的指令、cache、调度和 dispatch 不同；一个平台的格式支持或论文速度不能直接成为另一个平台的性能保证。\n\n## 诊断：格式、执行数值、表示近似与任务质量\n\n先固定比较对象，再逐层定位。\n\n1. 用独立解码器检查同一制品 P、S、Z 的 q、w_hat，以及逻辑轴和组参数映射。失败定位格式、参数身份或加载映射，不能解释为表示近似的正常代价。\n2. 用同一制品解码后的高精度算子参考与目标 kernel 比较。两侧使用相同量化表示，其差异反映执行语义、索引、单位或有限精度路径。先排除漏项、重复项和补偿错误，再评价声明数值路径的容差；这层差异不是量化表示损失。\n3. 用量化制品的独立算子参考与原浮点权重、激活的独立参考比较，输入和函数边界保持一致。这层衡量表示近似；同制品 kernel 通过不证明该近似足够小。\n\n随后另行评价固定前缀 logits、真实生成与任务质量。任务质量还受误差传播、校准分布和多种近似组合影响。上述归因与量化契约、数值参考、权重加载章节的所供范围衔接：加载映射错误属于同制品实现问题，kernel 与量化参考的差异不能转记为相对原模型的表示损失。\n\n| 观察 | 竞争解释 | 区分实验 | 反证后的修正 |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| shape 正确但解码错 | nibble 顺序、符号、行跨度、组轴或尺度导出改变 | 已知非对称字节、逐行唯一码、单独改变一组参数 | 先修格式与映射，不放宽近似误差预算 |\n| 单向量通过，多输出失败 | qw/qx 被标量覆盖、tile 范围错或未清零 | N=M=2、K=1 反例；多行多列短尾 tile；重复调用 | 修索引和初始化，不能归因于浮点重排 |\n| 同制品点积失败 | 漏零点、全局尺度、共同组映射错或中间精度不足 | 不同 G_w/G_x、不同尺度、非零零点，对比逐元素解码参考 | 解码相同则继续定位归约与数值路径 |\n| 奇数 K 或尾组失败 | padding 被消费、长度错或尾部借参 | 实际读取 packed 行，改变非零填充激活并记录消费范围；非规范尾 nibble 应拒绝 | 修长度和 mask，不让额外项进入容差 |\n| K=0 或多 tile bias 错 | Y 未初始化、epilogue 位于归约内部 | 非零 bias、K=0；改变 BK 与段数 | 初始化零归约结果，每输出一次 epilogue |\n| 大 K 突变 | 原始点积、补偿或窄中间和溢出 | 对真实码域运行准入，比较 131071/131072，改变初值与分段 | 修宽度或计划，不以最终抵消证明中间安全 |\n| 字节下降而时间不降 | 解包、J 增大、fallback、同步或线程争用 | 固定质量和 shape，分别改变 M、G、BK、线程并记录路径 | 观察不支持带宽瓶颈时撤回该归因 |\n| kernel 匹配量化参考，原模型差异大 | 表示近似、校准漂移、敏感层或 W/KV 组合 | 固定输入，分别恢复权重、激活、KV 或敏感层精度 | 只有相应干预有效时才缩小原因 |\n| API 接受但性能不变 | 内部转换、回退或其他资源主导 | 核对实际内核与指令，分开准备、稳态和服务 | 路径不符先修 dispatch，路径成立再查完整瓶颈 |\n\n负对照必须穿过被检机制。尾部检查让正确归约从 packed 行提取有效码，再让错误变体实际多读一个高 nibble；宽度检查运行返回准入、分段或拒绝的决策函数，再注入 127² 错界或取消拒绝。只比较预写的非零表达式与零，或只证明常数界大于 INT32_MAX，不能证明消费掩码或拒绝路径有效。\n\n随附 tools/check_k_fw_012_full.py 是标准库、确定性、离线、只读的软件算术模型。独立参考从逻辑码逐元素恢复实数，再计算稠密矩阵乘；被检路径读取 packed 行或执行矩阵 tile 与交段补偿。覆盖多行多列、不同组长、跨 BK、短尾、K=0 与 bias、空 N/M、已知字节、全部 INT4 码对、实际尾部归约、J 计数和整数准入/分段/拒绝。故障变体包括最后标量广播、错误初值、漏零点、全局尺度、额外消费尾 nibble、错误极值界及取消拒绝后的环绕。\n\n本轮实际完成了 JavaScript 软件交叉检查；交付 Python 脚本尚未执行，其运行结果须单独保存。两者均不执行目标 CPU/GPU kernel，不验证完整 IEEE 舍入、真实线程可见性、ISA、dispatch、cache/TLB、NUMA、模型质量或性能。检查数量不能替代这些范围说明。\n\n## 组合轨迹：一次分组 CPU decode 线性层\n\n设 N=M=1、K=5、G=3、BK=2，逻辑码为 (−8,−1,0,7,3)，两个组的 S=(1/4,1/2)、Z=(1,", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 4000], [8000, 14625]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 17656, "selected_characters": 10625, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "Ranked 4000-character windows by current entry title/tags/questions and algorithm/mechanism keywords; ties retain source order."}, {"path": "knowledge/engine-index/tensorrt-llm.md", "sha256": "6ade1253f3747041bef4a816d82387c3771bff3459a153719cfbd571c2454c0b", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "related_chapter", "content": "# K-ENGINE-005：异步执行决策：结果确认、在途状态与回收\n\n主机准备、设备计算、采样与外部状态传输可以部分重叠，但自回归依赖与存储生存期不会因异步提交而消失。设计需要区分业务完成、输出提交、设备完成和各个外部消费者结束。\n\n## 重叠怎样产生收益\n\n若 host 工作与设备工作互相独立且有足够缓冲，稳态周期可从 tₕ+t𝒹 接近 max(tₕ,t𝒹)；这不是单请求时延的通用公式。设定 tₕ=2 ms、t𝒹=5 ms，理想流水周期从 7 ms 降到 5 ms，最多约 1.4 倍的该段吞吐改善；实际同步、采样依赖或共享带宽会缩小重叠。\n\n提前安排下一代输入可以减少 host 等待，也会增加在途状态。取消到达时，尚未提交的可见输出可以被抑制，已经发出的设备工作则可能需要排空。同步确认每步更简单；允许多代在途可提高流水效率，但增加代次映射、预留和迟到结果处理成本。\n\n## 传输使用者与缓存回收\n\n[已核对的 TensorRT-LLM 实例](../sources/S-TRTLLM-CODE.md) 对请求完成后的异步发送/保存计数，在多个传输消费者均结束后解除相应保护；加载走其他路径。它说明一个完成标记不能代替所有使用者的证据，不证明该组件同时实现了所有驻留和恢复策略。\n\n```pseudocode\n开始一个将读取缓存的操作前，登记其身份并保护相关存储\n业务终止时停止生成新的可见输出，记录已有在途操作\n每个操作在约定完成点撤销自己的使用权；重复或旧代完成不能释放新代存储\n业务持有与所有实际消费者均结束后，决定保留缓存或使旧身份失效并复用存储\n异常时保存失败与未完成状态，按实际传输/设备语义决定排空和恢复\n```\n\n若两个发送者持有同一状态，一个完成后另一个仍未完成，保护不能因“收到一次完成”而撤销。设备 kernel、发送和存储可能使用不同完成证据；将它们统称为 future 完成会丢失回收条件。\n\n## 如何验证重叠而不牺牲语义\n\n固定相同逻辑输入，比较同步与重叠候选的有效产出、输出顺序、停止后额外工作、峰值状态和尾延迟。分别延迟采样、传输完成或取消通知，检查结果归属与保护是否仍成立。host 等待减少、设备空隙缩小但总时间不变，提示其他瓶颈接管；吞吐增加同时显著扩大状态驻留，则需要重新评价容量而非只报告单步收益。\n\n远端预取、SSD 驻留和故障恢复仍需各自的身份、预留、数据可见性与接管机制，分别见[多级驻留](../algorithms/tiered-residency.md)、[状态传输](../system-semantics/distributed-state-transfer.md)和[服务恢复](../system-semantics/serving-recovery.md)。\n\n<!-- padding 和 overlap 思路。\n  [完整原文](../../references/S-TRTLLM/reader.html) · [全文文本](../../references/S-TRTLLM/fulltext.txt)\n\n- [S-TRTLLM-CODE：TensorRT-LLM PyExecutor 固定提交](../sources/S-TRTLLM-CODE.md)：固定历史提交的 PyExecutor 包含调度准备、_schedule、_sample_async 和异步传输管理。AsyncTransferManager 在发送与保存的全部使用者完成后解除 KV 保护；加载状态由其他路径处理。\n "knowledge/frameworks/cuda-graph-buffers.md", "sha256": "1c08b6a70f287162611caabe1d7061b3b5195f72a59664778a1ef83d2d052272", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "related_chapter", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:4000]\n# K-FW-005：CUDA Graph：捕获边界、计划状态与变长服务\n\nCUDA Graph 将反复出现的设备工作及依赖预先定义、实例化，再通过重放减少逐个 kernel 的参数设置与提交。变长服务仍然动态：请求成员、query 数、页表和有效长度每步变化，需要由捕获外计划器、固定容量缓冲和明确的内容所有权承接。图降低提交成本，不自动减少模型算术、KV 读取、准备工作或同步责任。\n\n## 图固定的对象与可变内容\n\n记图为 `G=(V,E)`，节点描述 kernel、拷贝、memset、事件或其他受支持操作，边描述依赖；实例化得到可执行图 `E_G`。普通固定参数的 capture/replay 路径需要区分三类状态：\n\n- `A_G`：节点参数保存的虚拟地址，包括输入、metadata、输出和 workspace。未经受支持的更新，每次重放仍使用这些地址。\n- `κ_G`：捕获时确定的形状、stride、dtype、布局、kernel 变体、grid 和按值传递的标量。\n- `D_g`：第 g 代写入固定缓冲的内容，包括 token、position、有效长度、页索引、工作队列和输出行映射所依赖的数据。\n\n因此，固定地址中的 length tensor 可以保存不同长度；改写它并不改变节点的 grid，也不扩展页索引数组。修改 Python 整数不会重新执行捕获时的 CPU 分支。若某项长度变化要求换 kernel、改变按值传递的参数或扩大固定张量，必须换图、使用明确支持的更新，或退出图路径。\n\n可重放状态可写为 `(E_G,A_G,κ_G,D_g,g)`。输入是本轮请求快照及其所需状态，输出是张量结果、KV 副作用和与请求身份对应的行映射。正确性同时要求：特化契约相容；所有被访问地址有效；生产写入先于读取；读者看到同一代内容；结果按该代映射解释；最后使用前不回收。地址稳定只解决其中的引用条件。\n\n## 捕获、重放与内存共享的合法边界\n\n固定 PyTorch 文档要求在非默认 stream 上捕获，先在 side stream 预热；捕获期间不得发生 CPU/GPU 同步，CPU 工作不会被记录，重放会省略这些工作。同一进程同时只能有一个 capture，期间不能运行未捕获的 CUDA 工作。普通数据相关分支不能直接捕获；所给版本明确支持通过 `torch.cond()` 表达的特定设备条件路径，但不能据此推断任意 Python 控制流或任意编译后端均受支持。形状和布局仍须满足所选路径的契约。\n\n多 stream capture 的依赖图必须从初始 stream 分叉，并在结束前汇合回初始 stream；捕获事件的等待关系也须符合对应 capture graph 的规则。重放可以在其他 stream 上启动，但图内部可按依赖重新安排独立节点，不能把捕获时的 stream 编号当作固定硬件通道。\n\n重放还受以下独立条件约束：\n\n1. 同一个 `graphExec` 不能与自身并发执行，后续 launch 排在该实例先前 launch 之后。真正允许两批重叠需要不同可执行实例，并隔离冲突的可变存储；不同 stream 本身不能提供这种隔离。\n2. PyTorch 默认为每次 capture 建立私有池。固定文档给出的一个安全共享情形是：图总按捕获顺序重放，且从不并发。文档还明确允许互不依赖对方输出的独立图共享池，只要它们从不并发；这一情形不要求总按捕获顺序选择图桶。\n3. 共享池中的一次重放可能覆盖另一图的输出。相关消费者必须在覆盖前完成，或先把需要保留的输出复制到受保护的独立存储；异步复制也要完成后才能覆盖源。独立图仍可能因外部共享 KV、权重 staging 或 workspace 产生冲突，池的共享许可不消除这些依赖。\n4. CUDA 图内存节点是另一层机制：分配节点的虚拟地址固定，实际分配具有 GPU 顺序生命期，不重叠的分配可复用底层物理页。固定地址不保证内容跨 free 持久存在。不能把 PyTorch 私有池的共享条件推广成所有图内存复用的统一规则。\n5. 图更新有接口限制。整图更新需要符合拓扑、依赖顺序和节点对应规则；逐节点更新也有类型、函数上下文和内存类型限制。更新成功不证明新地址的内容版本正确，也不替代在途生存期保护。\n\n完整捕获减少更多 host 提交；分段捕获保留动态计划、CPU grammar 或输出处理边界，但增加图间提交及中间状态。选择取决于提交开销的位置和跨段状态大小。若采样也进入图，随机数状态还须按框架的图安全接口维护，请求重排不能无意改变服务承诺的随机性归属。\n\n## 请求布局决定桶是否可行\n\n桶的签名、每请求上界和整批容量是三个不同对象。\n\n签名 `σ_b` 包括真正影响捕获参数或执行语义的项目，例如 mode、dtype、head 几何、页长、stride、attention backend、mask 实现、LoRA 路径和投机变体。某项如果由 kernel 通过固定 metadata 间接读取，可以作为内容变化；若被特化为常量或具体指针，就必须进入签名或绑定关系。相同 rank 不代表 adapter 身份相同。\n\n对有确定顺序的真实请求集合 `R=(r₀,…,rₙ₋₁)`，记 `q_i` 为本轮 query 行数，`p_i` 为该布局中请求 i 占用的页索引条目数。页索引条目数不等于唯一物理 KV 页数：两个请求即使引用同一前缀页，各自的索引区间仍可能各占一个条目。物理容量账则只计该共享页一次。\n\n本节选择一种可独立实现的压紧布局：\n\n`u₀=v₀=0`，`uᵢ₊₁=uᵢ+q_i`，`vᵢ₊₁=vᵢ+p_i`。\n\n请求 i 的 query 放在 `[u_i,uᵢ₊₁)`，页索引放在 `[v_i,vᵢ₊₁)`。行 `u_i+j` 对应 `(请求身份,请求代次,绝对位置或分支 j)`。整批需求为 `n` 个请求、`Q=u_n` 行、`P=v_n` 个页索引。偏移单位分别是 query 行和页索引位置，末页有效长度则以 token 计，不能混用。\n\n| 对象 | 桶记录的约束 | 判定方式 |\n|---|---|---|\n| 签名 | `σ_b` | 每个请求及计划所选变体都相容 |\n| 单请求 | `q_i≤qmax_b`、`p_i≤pmax_b`，以及历史、tail、mask 等有效域 | 逐请求检查 |\n| 整批固定数组 | 请求容量 `N_b`、query 容量 `Q_b`、页索引容量 `P_b` | `n≤N_b`、`Q≤Q_b`、`P≤P_b` 同时成立 |\n| 派生计划 | 队列条目、mask 字节、partial output 等固定区段 | 由实际计划计算需求后逐区段检查 |\n| 物理峰值 | KV、固定缓冲、图池、workspace 与在途副本 | 按同时存活物理存储的并集检查 |\n\n这里的消费契约是：设备端有效计数及队列长度控制实际任务；固定长度 `qo_indptr[N_b+1]`、`kv_indptr[N_b+1]` 在真实请求之后重复末偏移；固定 `Q_b` 行张量的尾部不属于真实请求。attention 和 KV 写入按有效范围执行，稠密层若仍计算尾部，其输出丢弃。这样不需要把每个尾部张量行伪装成真实请求。\n\n这是一种带有效范围保护的布局选择，不是对所有现成 wrapper 的保证。有的 backend 不接受零长尾段，或要求分段末端严格覆盖整个 query 张量；此时须采用它支持的 dummy 请求布局，重新计算包含 dummy 的请求数、query 数、页索引数和全部偏移。dummy 写入必须跳过或使用与真实状态隔离的写位置；多个填充写者也不能无协议地竞争同一写槽。仅在 host 上标记“无效行”不能阻止设备写入。\n\n设 `N_b=2,Q_b=64,P_b=16,qmax_b=64`。两个同签名请求各需 40 行、4 个页索引，逐请求都合法，但 `u=[0,40,80]`、`v=[0,4,8]`，整批需要 80 行，桶不可行。可拆成两个单请求批，各自 `u=[0,40]`；若有其他满足所有容量的桶也可改选。不能截断到 64 行或仅靠“显存还有空间”放行。类似地，请求数和页索引总数可各自独立越界，即使总 query 行数合法。\n\n## 桶、槽与可执行图沿同一绑定推进\n\n本章采用逐槽捕获方案。桶 b 的第 s 个槽拥有固定地址集合\n\n`A_(b,s)=(input,metadata,plan,output,workspace)`，\n\n并捕获、实例化对应的 `E_(b,s)`。初始化时用该槽的合法占位内容预热，在绑定地址上捕获执行段；实例化成功、缓冲保活和资源登记完成后，才把这一组合加入可用集合。不同槽需要不同的捕获地址；复制一个仍引用 s₀ 地址的图模板，不会自动得到引用 s₁ 的图。\n\n这里的槽标识包含桶身份。共享只读权重或稳定 KV arena 的基址可以相同，具体页选择来自该槽 metadata；可并发槽的输入、metadata、输出和可变 workspace 必须隔离。图内部临时区若来自共享私有池，还须服从上一节的串行条件。本章的并发方案为这些可变区使用独立存储和相容的分配器安排。\n\n```pseudocode\n算法 1：准备并提交一个固定绑定的批次\n输入：有序请求快照 R；已就绪的绑定集合 (b,s,E_(b,s),A_(b,s))\n输出：NOOP；或执行记录 H；或明确的拆分、回退、拒绝原因\n\n1. 检查每个请求的输入、版本、页表和上游生产依赖。\n   未就绪请求保留等\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 8000:14625]\n1≤2` 被拒绝，原内容不变。若只更新 `gen` 而不更新 `last_published_gen`，这条轨迹就会让旧代重新通过，读者结束并不能修复它。\n\n设一代从取得槽到最后使用结束的半开区间为 `[start_g,end_g)`，最少槽数受最大重叠代数约束：\n\n`N_s ≥ max_t |{g : start_g≤t<end_g}|`。\n\n若周期为 P、每代固定占用 L，且结束时可立即复用，则至少需要 `ceil(L/P)` 个槽；为提供可执行存储，零生存期模型仍保留一个槽。L 必须含准备写入、在途执行及输出消费者等待。长尾到达不能用平均 L 和平均 P 保证安全；实际复用仍由完成事件门控。增加槽只提供容量，不消除 token 反馈依赖或设备资源瓶颈。\n\n## 一次重叠与取消的组合轨迹\n\n设同一桶有两个独立绑定 `(s₀,E₀)`、`(s₁,E₁)`，各自使用独立可变 workspace。两批访问不同请求的可写 KV，公共权重只读。以下是逻辑事件轨迹，不是 GPU 实测。\n\n| 事件 | 内容与访问责任 | 允许的后继 |\n|---|---|---|\n| s₀ 发布 g=2，提交 E₀ | s₀ 的输入、metadata、输出和 workspace 受保护；KV 引用进入执行记录 | 可准备 s₁，不能覆盖 s₀ |\n| s₁ 发布 g=3，提交 E₁ | E₁ 的捕获参数全部指向 s₁；独立存储允许执行重叠，但不保证硬件实际重叠 | 两批分别按完成依赖推进 |\n| g=2 中请求取消 | 停止新业务提交和交付；E₀ 已发出的读写仍有责任 | 不立即回收其 KV，不将 s₀ 交给新批 |\n| E₀ 完成，输出复制尚未结束 | 图责任结束，复制仍读 s₀.output | s₀ 保持 DRAINING |\n| 输出复制完成并丢弃取消结果 | 该代所有责任结束 | s₀ 回到 FREE，高水位仍为 2 |\n| g=1 的迟到准备到达 s₀ | 门控拒绝，未写入任何固定区 | E₁ 和 s₀ 有效状态均不受影响 |\n| s₀ 接受更新代 g=4，随后收到 g=2 的重复完成 | 重复完成不匹配当前代 | 不得释放 g=4 的责任或 KV |\n\n两槽隔离不保护外部共享可写 KV。如果两批属于同一自回归请求，或写同一尾页，仍需 token 依赖、写时复制或明确的读写顺序。取消通知只撤销业务意图，不证明 GPU 已停止。仅递增请求代次也不能阻止旧 DMA 写坏已经重新分配的物理地址。\n\n卸载权重使用固定 staging 地址时，换入必须完成后图才能读取；最后读取结束后才能换入下一层或版本。LoRA 若捕获具体 adapter 指针，切换需另一绑定、受支持更新或固定槽内容替换。较小 rank 填入较大 rank 桶时，新增位置的数学贡献必须为零，scale、routing 和 A/B 版本须一致。固定地址不能掩盖混合权重版本。\n\n## 提交、准备、交通与驻留成本\n\n设 eager 每轮提交 K 个短 kernel，每次主机提交及分派成本约 h，图重放主机成本约 g，则可减少的主机工作约为 `Kh−g`。它只有位于关键路径时才等量改善墙钟。主机工作 H 与设备工作 D 可充分重叠时，稳态周期近似受 `max(H,D)` 及反馈依赖约束；单请求必须等待结果再准备下一 token 时，相应链仍串行。\n\n设定 `K=100,h=8 μs,g=0.05 ms`，主机项从约 0.8 ms 降至 0.05 ms。若 D=1 ms 且这些项无法隐藏，总时间粗略从 1.8 ms 降为 1.05 ms；D=20 ms 时则从 20.8 ms 降为 20.05 ms。它们是设定计算，不是设备测量。图不改变长 GEMM 的算术量，也不自动减少 HBM 中的权重与 KV 字节。\n\n固定容量的额外设备成本来自真实执行范围。稠密层处理全部 `Q_b` 行时，padding 增量通常近似随 `Q_b−Q` 增长；按有效队列执行的 attention 不一定计算全部尾部行，但仍付出固定 grid、队列读取和无任务 CTA 的成本。KV 长度、head 分组、页表访问和 split 合并决定实际工作，不能用一个 padding 行比例直接预测时间。若图少花 0.2 ms 提交，却增加 0.5 ms 执行，当前桶的直接收益为负。\n\n准备包括快照、前缀和、映射、mask 打包、变体选择和任务分配。设 W 个任务、C 个 CTA，先排序再用堆分配的一种计划器需要约 `O(W log W+W log C)` 的 CPU 工作，另有 `O(n+Q+P)` 的输入与索引整理；具体实现可选其他组织。计划复制 `B_plan` 字节至少承担对应方向的链路交通和启动成本，后续设备队列读取还消耗设备访存。一个 plan 可被 L 个相容层复用时，准备及复制工作按层摊销约为 `(c_meta+c_copy)/L`；每步都变化的计划不能跨步摊销。\n\n同步成本既包括事件与责任账，也包括准备未就绪、槽忙、输出消费者背压所造成的等待。没有把旧读者排空，多槽容量模型就不成立；把所有工作全设备同步虽可能消除竞态，却会改变重叠和关键路径。跨设备通信若进入图，传输字节仍存在，参与者、顺序与缓冲责任仍须一致，图不能消除通信协议成本。\n\n捕获冷路径单列：预热、库初始化、分配、capture、实例化及必要 upload。逐槽捕获使构建对象数随合法 `(b,s)` 组合增加。若总新增准备成本为 C₀，每次相同语义调用净省 Δt>0，则单计时间摊销需 `N>C₀/Δt`；少用或频繁失效的图可能永不回本。首请求等待和部署就绪也不能从稳态均值中消失。\n\n物理驻留按同时存活存储的并集计量：权重、唯一 KV、固定输入/输出、plan、workspace、图池保留、在途源与未排空旧版本。workspace 若已属于某个图池保留区域，不能再把其完整 bytes 加一次。共享串行池可降低占用；并发槽的可变区需要真实隔离，通常增加容量。\n\n设定四个桶各有互不重叠的额外图池 64 MiB，合计 256 MiB；KV 每 token 占 128 KiB 时，直接机会成本是 2,048 token。若再增加两个未包含在上述池中的独立 workspace，各 8 MiB，另相当于 128 token，合计 2,176 token。这个算例不表示真实图的固定大小。增加桶减少 padding，也可能挤掉 KV、降低准入并扩大排队，因此最小可行桶不一定给出最低服务成本。\n\n## 条件变化后的保留、调整与放弃\n\n| 条件变化 | 保留的约束 | 调整候选 | 推翻当前选择的信号 |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| batch 成员或 query 数变化 | 重建聚合容量、偏移和输出映射 | 相容桶、拆批、普通执行 | 单请求均合法而总行、总页索引或请求数越界 |\n| 长度分布长尾增加 | 不截断历史或页表 | 高频形状图化，长尾分块或回退 | 构建与驻留增加，复用次数不足以摊销 |\n| adapter、dtype、backend 改变 | 特化与内容身份均相容 | 新绑定、合法更新或稳定间接选择 | 地址仍有效但版本、布局或变体失配 |\n| 增加执行重叠 | 全部可变区与外部状态受保护 | 增加 E_(b,s) 和独立 workspace，或串行化 | 增槽只扩大驻留与等待，或共享区污染 |\n| 取消与输出背压增加 | 最后物理使用前不回收 | 缩短在途、调整消费者和复制边界 | 无效执行、排空时间和占槽超过新增有效产出 |\n| 显存紧张 | 容量按物理并集计量 | 减桶、减槽、共享相容串行池 | kernel 更快但准入下降、驱逐或 OOM 增加 |\n| host 已不在关键路径 | 计算与交通仍存在 | 保留低开销路径或简化为 eager | host 时间下降而完整 ITL 几乎不变 |\n| 固定 grid 负载不均 | 任务完整覆盖且计划有界 | 调整 CTA、切分和合并映射 | 空 CTA、长尾或合并成本抵消提交收益 |\n\n这些是有前提的候选，不要求服务必须采用多槽、更多桶或完整捕获。仅一条执行链且并发收益有限时，单绑定加串行事件保护可能更简单；独立桶从不并发时，可以利用文档允许的私有池共享。\n\n## 反例与区分实验\n\n先固定逻辑输入、数值契约、有效工作和计时范围，再改变可区分的条件。结构正确不能由性能提升抵消，模型检查也不能替代设备可见性验证。\n\n| 竞争解释或具体错误 | 干预与独立观测 | 可区分预测及修正 |\n|---|---|---|\n| 桶越界源于漏算聚合需求 | 给 64 行桶两个各 40 行请求；用逐行写入有界数组的参考检查 | 逐请求判定接受而参考越界；修正为布局聚合判定并拆批 |\n| 重放读取了另一槽 | 两槽写不同 token、metadata 和代次，刻意启动错误绑定 | 错图读取捕获槽的数据；增加槽数无效，须修正 E 与地址绑定 |\n| 迟到旧代覆盖新代 | 先发布 2，完成所有消费者，再提交 1；比较拒绝前后快照 | 高水位正确维护时拒绝且不写入；缺少更新会覆盖 |\n| 只等待图完成，漏掉输出消费者 | 延迟输出复制完成，再尝试下一代写入 | 图已完成仍不能覆盖输出；应延长槽责任或复制到独立受保护区 |\n| 提交开销位于关键路径 | 相同张量、KV、同步点下切换 eager/图，分记 host、设备和墙钟 | host API 与间隙缩小而 kernel 工作近似不变；墙钟不变则重新定位瓶颈 |\n| padding 或驻留抵消收益 | 相同 offered workload 比较粗细桶，记录执行行、物理峰值、准入和尾延迟 | 细桶执行更少却可能因驻留导致服务变差，不能只报告 replay 时间 |\n| plan 准备或复制主导 | 固定张量形状，仅改变成员、页表或 mask，分记 plan 与执行 | replay 不变而准备增长，提示准备缓存或表示需要调整 |\n| 共享 workspace 的内容竞争 | 比较独立 workspace 与明确事件串行化，保留相同输入 | 两种方式都消错支持共享区时序解释；仍错误则检查绑定、映射或数值 |\n| 固定 grid 负载不均 | 固定任务集合改变 CTA 数，记录空任务、最长队列和合并时间 | 更多 CTA 只增合并而不缩短关键队列时应停止增加 |\n| 捕获覆盖不足 | 查看整步时间线，调整稳定段的捕获边界 | 图外控制仍留空隙，收益受未捕获关键链限制 |\n\n容量、绑定和迟到代次反例可以用 CPU 离散模型触发。host/device 时间线、stream/event 可见性、真实并发和性能则需要目标设备实验。测量应记录预热、频率、并发、共享带宽和同步点，不能把 host 返回时间或一次 kernel 区间当作完整服务收益。\n\n## 验证范围\n\n随章 `tools/check_k_fw_005_full.py` 是确定、离线、只读的 Python 标准库模型。它用有界数组逐项放置作为独立布局参考，枚举小批容量边界；另外检查固定捕获地址、发布高水位、多消费者门控、旧完成隔离、槽数、填充写入隔离和 attention 合并。故意保留“只逐请求判断”“启动另一槽的图”“不更新高水位”“少一个槽”和“忽略无效行”等负对照，要求它们实际触发不变量失败。\n\n脚本不调用 CUDA，不证明真实 capture 合法性、图更新支持、设备内存可见性、驱动分配或性能。本轮未执行该 Python 文件。真实实现还需核对重复捕获/释放、跨桶切换、超容量回退、取消后迟到事件、权重或 adapter 换入、共享池输出覆盖及峰值存储；设备数值与资源结果应使用独立参考和真实完成记录。\n\n<!-- infraagents:provenance -->\n\n## 来源与推导\n\n- [S-TORCH-CUDA：PyTorch — CUDA semantics](../sources/S-TORCH-CUDA.md)：固定文档说明异步提交、跨 stream 的执行依赖与分配器生存期，以及 CUDA Graph 的固定参数、地址、形状和捕获限制。所供原文还明确允许互不依赖输出的独立图在不并发时共享私有池；按捕获顺序且不并发是另一安全情形。共享池仍可能覆盖其他图的输出。本章依据原文补足来源说明对共享情形的概括，不修改共享来源说明。\n  [完整原文](../../references/S-TORCH-CUDA/reader.html) · ===\nHigh-throughput Generative Inference of Large Language Models\nwith a Single GPU\nYing Sheng 1 Lianmin Zheng 2 Binhang Yuan 3 Zhuohan Li 2 Max Ryabinin 4 5 Daniel Y. Fu 1 Zhiqiang Xie 1\nBeidi Chen 6 7 Clark Barrett 1 Joseph E. Gonzalez 2 Percy Liang 1 Christopher R´e 1 Ion Stoica 2 Ce Zhang 3\nAbstract\nThe high computational and memory requirements of large language model (LLM) inference traditionally make it\nfeasible only with multiple high-end accelerators. Motivated by the emerging demand for latency-insensitive tasks\nwith batched processing, this paper initiates the study of high-throughput LLM inference using limited resources,\nsuch as a single commodity GPU. We present FlexGen, a high-throughput generation engine for running LLMs\nwith limited GPU memory. FlexGen can be ﬂexibly conﬁgured under various hardware resource constraints by\naggregating memory and computation from the GPU, CPU, and disk. Through a linear programming optimizer,\nit searches for efﬁcient patterns to store and access tensors. FlexGen further compresses these weights and the\nattention cache to 4 bits with negligible accuracy loss. These techniques enable FlexGen to have a larger space of\nbatch size choices and thus signiﬁcantly increase maximum throughput. As a result, when running OPT-175B on\na single 16GB GPU, FlexGen achieves signiﬁcantly higher throughput compared to state-of-the-art ofﬂoading\nsystems, reaching a generation throughput of 1 token/s for the ﬁrst time with an effective batch size of 144. On\nthe HELM benchmark, FlexGen can benchmark a 30B model with a 16GB GPU on 7 representative sub-scenarios\nin 21 hours. The code is available at https://github.com/FMInference/FlexGen.\n1. Introduction\nIn recent years, large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong performance across a wide range of tasks (Brown\net al., 2020; Bommasani et al., 2021; Zhang et al., 2022; Chowdhery et al., 2022). Along with these unprecedented\ncapabilities, generative LLM inference comes with unique challenges. These models can have billions, if not trillions\nof parameters (Chowdhery et al., 2022; Fedus et al., 2022), which leads to extremely high computational and memory\nrequirements to run. For example, GPT-175B requires 325GB of GPU memory simply to load its model weights. Fitting\nthis model onto GPUs would require at least ﬁve A100 (80GB) GPUs and complex parallelism strategies (Pope et al., 2022;\nAminabadi et al., 2022). Thus, lowering LLM inference resource requirements has recently attracted intense interest.\nIn this paper, we focus on a setting that we call throughput-oriented generative inference. In addition to interactive use\ncases such as chatbots, LLMs are also applied to many “back-of-house” tasks such as benchmarking (Liang et al., 2022),\ninformation extraction (Narayan et al., 2018), data wrangling (Narayan et al., 2022), and form processing (Chen et al.,\n2021). One key characteristic of these tasks is that they often require running LLM inference in batches over a large number\nof tokens (e.g., all the documents in a company’s corpus), and are less sensitive to the latency of token generation. As a\nresult, it is possible to trade off latency for higher throughput in these workloads, providing opportunities to reduce resource\nrequirements.\nPrior efforts to lower resource requirements of LLM inference correspond to three directions: (1) model compression\nto decrease total memory footprint (Dettmers et al., 2022; Yao et al., 2022; Frantar et al., 2022; Xiao et al., 2022); (2)\ncollaborative inference to amortize inference cost via decentralization (Borzunov et al., 2022); and (3) ofﬂoading to utilize\nmemory from CPU and disk (Aminabadi et al., 2022; HuggingFace, 2022). These techniques have signiﬁcantly lowered the\nresource requirements for using LLMs, but there are distinct limitations. Research in the ﬁrst two directions often assume\nthat the model ﬁts into the GPU memory and thereby struggle to run 175B-scale models with a single commodity GPU. On\nthe other hand, state-of-the-art ofﬂoading-based systems in the third category do not achieve acceptable throughput on a\nsingle GPU due to inefﬁcient I/O scheduling and tensor placement. For example, these systems can be bottlenecked by\nsmall batch sizes (e.g., batch sizes of only one or two for OPT-175B in some cases).\n1Stanford University 2UC Berkeley 3ETH Zurich 4Yandex 5HSE University 6Meta 7Carnegie Mellon University.\nYing Sheng <ying1123@stanford.edu>, Lianmin Zheng <lian-\nminzheng@gmail.com>, Binhang Yuan <biyuan@inf.ethz.ch>, Zhuohan Li <zhuohan@cs.berkeley.edu>, Max Ryabinin <mryabinin0@gmail.com>, Daniel Y. The latency and throughput trade-offs of three ofﬂoading-based systems for OPT-175B (left) and OPT-30B (right) on a single\nNVIDIA T4 (16 GB) GPU with 208 GB CPU DRAM. FlexGen achieves a new Pareto-optimal frontier with 100× higher maximum\nthroughput for OPT-175B. Other systems cannot further increase throughput due to out-of-memory issues. “(c)” denotes compression.\n16 GB\n208 GB\n1.5 TB\nGPU\nCPU\nDisk\n12 GB/s\n2 GB/s\nOur focus is designing efﬁcient ofﬂoading strategies for high-throughput generative inference, on a\nsingle commodity GPU. To run an LLM with limited GPU memory, we can ofﬂoad it to secondary\nstorage and perform computation part-by-part by partially loading it. On a typical machine, there\nare three levels of the memory hierarchy, as illustrated in the ﬁgure to the right. Higher levels are\nfaster but scarce, while lower levels are slower but abundant. In throughput-oriented scenarios, we\ncan sacriﬁce latency by using a large batch size, and amortize the expensive I/O operations among\ndifferent memory hierarchies over a large batch of inputs, overlapped with computation. Fig. 1 shows the latency-throughput\ntrade-off of three inference systems with ofﬂoading on a single NVIDIA T4 (16 GB) GPU. Note that the performance in\nterms of latency and throughput on limited resources is signiﬁcantly inferior to that of the cases with sufﬁcient resources.\nAchieving high-throughput generative inference with limited GPU memory is challenging even if we can sacriﬁce the\nlatency. The ﬁrst challenge is to design an efﬁcient ofﬂoading strategy. During generative inference, there are three kinds\nof tensors: weights, activations, and key-value (KV) cache. The strategy should specify what tensors to ofﬂoad, where to\nofﬂoad them within the three-level memory hierarchy, and when to ofﬂoad them during inference. The batch-by-batch,\ntoken-by-token, and layer-by-layer structure of the computation forms a complex dependency graph where there are multiple\nways to conduct computation. Together, these choices form a complex design space. Existing ofﬂoading-based inference\nsystems (Aminabadi et al., 2022; HuggingFace, 2022) inherit strategies from training, which turn out to be some suboptimal\npoints for inference, performing excessive I/O and achieving throughput far below theoretical hardware limits.\nThe second challenge is to develop effective compression strategies. Previous works have demonstrated promising results\nin compressing the weights and activations of LLMs. However, when combining compression with ofﬂoading for high-\nthroughput generative inference, the I/O costs and memory reduction of the weights and KV cache become more important,\nmotivating alternative compression schemes.\nTo address these challenges, we present FlexGen, an ofﬂoading framework for high-throughput LLM inference. FlexGen\naggregates memory from the GPU, CPU, and disk, and efﬁciently schedules I/O operations, along with possible compression\nmethods and distributed pipeline parallelism.\n(Contribution 1) We formally deﬁne a search space of possible ofﬂoading strategies by considering computation schedule,\ntensor placement, and computation delegation. We prove that our search space captures a computation order with I/O\ncomplexity within 2× of optimality. We then develop a linear programming-based search algorithm to optimize the\nthroughput within the search space. This algorithm can be conﬁgured for various hardware speciﬁcations and can be easily\nextended to incorporate latency and throughput constraints, thus helping to navigate the trade-off space smoothly. Compared\nwith existing strategies, our solution uniﬁes the placement of weights, activations, and the KV cache, enabling a dramatically\nhigher batch size upper bound, which is key to achieving high throughput.\n(Contribution 2) We show that it is possible to compress both the weights and KV cache for LLMs like OPT-175B to 4\nbits without retraining or calibration, all with negligible accuracy loss. This is achieved through ﬁne-grained group-wise\nquantization (Shen et al., 2020), which is suitable for reducing I/O costs and memory usage during ofﬂoading.\n(Contribution 3) We demonstrate the efﬁciency of FlexGen by running OPT-175B on NVIDIA T4 (16GB) GPUs. Compared\nto DeepSpeed Zero-Inference (Aminabadi et al., 2022) and Hugging Face Accelerate (HuggingFace, 2022), two state-of-the-\nart ofﬂoading-based inference systems, FlexGen often allows a batch size that is orders of magnitude larger. As a result,\nFlexGen can achieve much higher throughputs. On a single T4 GPU with 208 GB CPU DRAM and 1.5 TB SSD, input\n2\n\n\n=== PDF page 3 ===\nsequence length 512, and output sequence length 32:\n• With the same latency of 5000 seconds, FlexGen (effective batch size 64, or 2048 tokens in total) can achieve more\nthan 40× higher throughput than DeepSpeed Zero-Inference (batch size 1, or 32 tokens in total), while Hugging Face\nAccelerate cannot complete a single batch.\n• By allowing a higher latency of 12000 seconds, FlexGen achieves 69× higher maximum throughput compared to\nbaselines because it can enlarge the effective batch size to 256 (8192 tokens generated in total), while DeepSpeed\nZero-Inference and Hugging Face Accelerate cannot use a batch size larger than 2 due to out-of-memory issues.\n• If allowing 4-bit compression, FlexGen can reach 100× higher maximum throughput with effective batch size 144\n(4608 tokens generated in total) with latency 4000 seconds by holding all weights in CPU and getting rid of disk\nofﬂoading.\nWe also compare ofﬂoading and decentralized collective inference based on FlexGen and Petals (Borzunov et al., 2022) as\ntwo representative systems. We conduct comparisons between the two systems from the aspects of delay and bandwidth of\nthe decentralized network and output sequence length. The results show that FlexGen outperforms a decentralized Petals\ncluster in terms of per-GPU throughput and can even achieve lower latency in certain cases.\n2. Related Work\nGiven the recent advances of LLMs, LLM inference has become an important workload, encouraging active research from\nboth the system side and the algorithm side.\nRecent years have witnessed the emergence of systems specialized for LLM inference, such as FasterTransformer (NVIDIA,\n2022), Orca (Yu et al., 2022), LightSeq (Wang et al., 2021), PaLM inference (Pope et al., 2022), TurboTransformers (Fang\net al., 2021), DeepSpeed Inference (Aminabadi et al., 2022), and Hugging Face Accelerate (HuggingFace, 2022). Unfortu-\nnately, most of these systems focus on latency-oriented scenarios with high-end accelerators, limiting their deployment\nfor throughput-oriented inference on easily accessible hardware. To enable LLM inference on such commodity hardware,\nofﬂoading is an essential technique — as far as we know, among current systems, only DeepSpeed Zero-Inference and\nHugging Face Accelerate support ofﬂoading. These inference systems typically inherit the ofﬂoading techniques from\ntraining systems (Rajbhandari et al., 2021; Ren et al., 2021; Li et al., 2022; Huang et al., 2020; Wang et al., 2018) but ignore\nthe special computational property of generative inference. They fail to exploit the structure of the throughput-oriented\nLLM inference computation and miss great opportunities for efﬁcient scheduling of I/O trafﬁc. Another attempt to enable\nLLM inference on accessible hardware is collaborative computing proposed by Petals (Borzunov et al., 2022).\nBesides these advances on the system side, there are also many algorithm-oriented works that relax certain aspects of\ncomputation in LLM inference to accelerate the computation or reduce the memory footprint. Both sparsiﬁcation (Hoeﬂer\net al., 2021; Frantar & Alistarh, 2023) and quantization (Kwon et al., 2022; Yao et al., 2022; Park et al., 2022; Xiao et al.,\n2022; Frantar et al., 2022; Dettmers et al., 2022) have been adopted for LLM inference. On the quantization side, prior\nworks have shown weights can be compressed down to 3 bits without compressing activations (Frantar et al., 2022), or both\nweights and activations can be compressed to 8 bits (Yao et al., 2022; Dettmers et al., 2022; Xiao et al., 2022). In FlexGen,\nwe compress both the weights and KV cache to 4 bits and show how to combine the compression with ofﬂoading to make\nfurther improvements.\nWithin domains outside of LLM inference, memory optimizations and ofﬂoading have been studied for training (Huang\net al., 2020; Ren et al., 2021; Steiner et al., 2022) and linear algebra (Jia-Wei & Kung, 1981; Demmel, 2013).\n3. Background: LLM Inference\nIn this section, we describe the LLM inference workﬂow and its memory footprint.\nGenerative Inference. A typical LLM generative inference task consists of two stages: i) the preﬁll stage which takes a\nprompt sequence to generate the key-value cache (KV cache) for each transformer layer of the LLM; and ii) the decoding\nstage which utilizes and updates the KV cache to generate tokens step-by-step, where the current token generation depends\non previously generated tokens. Formally, the computation of a transformer layer can be summarized below:\nFor a particular inference computation, denote the batch size by b, the input sequence length by s, the output sequence\nlength by n, the hidden dimension of the transformer by h1, the hidden dimension of the second MLP layer by h2, and the\n3\n\n\n=== PDF page 4 ===\ntotal number of transformer layers by l. Given the weight matrices of a transformer layer speciﬁed by wi\nK, wi\nQ, wi\nV , wi\nO,\nwi\n1, wi\n2, where wi\nK, wi\nQ, wi\nV , wi\nO ∈Rh1×h1, w1 ∈Rh1×h2, and w2 ∈Rh2×h1.\nDuring the preﬁll phase, the input of the i-th layer is speciﬁed by xi, and key, value, query, and output of the\nattention layer is speciﬁed by xi\nK, xi\nV , xi\nQ, xi\nOut, where xi, xi\nK, xi\nV , xi\nQ, xi\nOut ∈Rb×s×h1. Then, the cached key, value\ncan be computed by:\nxi\nK = xi · wi\nK;\nxi\nV = xi · wi\nV\nThe rest of the computation in the i-th layer is:\nxi\nQ = xi · wi\nQ\nxi\nOut = fSoftmax\n\u0012\nxi\nQxi\nK\nT\n√\nh\n\u0013\n· xi\nV · wi\nO + xi\nxi+1 = frelu\n\u0000xi\nOut · w1\n\u0001\n· w2 + xi\nOut\nDuring the decoding phase, given the embedding of the current generated token in the i-th layer noted by ti ∈Rb×1×h1,\nthe inference computation needs to i) update the KV cache; and ii) compute the output of the current layer. The update of\nthe KV cache can be formalized as:\nxi\nK ←Concat\n\u0000xi\nK, ti · wi\nK\n\u0001\nxi\nV ←Concat\n\u0000xi\nV , ti · wi\nV\n\u0001\nThe rest of the computation for the current layer is:\nti\nQ = ti · wi\nQ\nti\nOut = fSoftmax\n\u0012\nti\nQxi\nK\nT\n√\nh\n\u0013\n· xi\nV · wi\nO + ti\nti+1 = frelu\n\u0000ti\nOut · w1\n\u0001\n· w2 + ti\nOut\nMemory Analysis. The memory footprint of LLM inference mainly comes from two components: the model weights and\nthe KV cache. Considering the OPT-175B model in FP16, the total number of bytes to store the parameters can be roughly\n1 calculated by l(8h2\n1 + 4h1h2). The total number of bytes to store the KV cache in peak is 4 × blh1(s + n).\nIn a realistic setting with a sufﬁcient number of GPUs, the OPT-175B model (l = 96, h1 = 12288, h2 = 49152) takes 325\nGB. With a batch size of b = 512, an input sequence length s = 512, and an output sequence length of n = 32, the total\nmemory required to store the KV cache is 1.2 TB, which is 3.8× the model weights, making the KV cache a new bottleneck\nof large-batch high-throughput inference. In the single GPU setting, the batch size is usually smaller but is still a signiﬁcant\nbottleneck. In FlexGen, for OPT-175B, we enlarge the effective batch size to 256 to achieve the throughput at 0.69 token/s.\nThroughput and Latency. Considering an effective batch size b, an input sequence length s, and an output sequence length\nof n, the latency t is deﬁned as the total number of seconds spent to process the prompts and generate all the bn tokens. The\ngeneration throughput is deﬁned as bn/t token/s.\nToken 0\nToken 1\nToken 2\nlayer\nDataset\n(infinite)\n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \nbatch\nFigure 2. Computational graph of LLM inference.\n4. Ofﬂoading Strategy\nIn this section, we do not relax any computation of LLM inference and illustrate how to formalize the ofﬂoading procedure\nunder the GPU, CPU, and disk memory hierarchy. We ﬁrst formulate the problem and then construct the search space of\nthe possible ofﬂoading strategies in FlexGen. To ﬁnd an efﬁcient strategy, FlexGen builds an analytical cost model and\nsearches for conﬁgurations with an optimizer based on linear programming. We also show how to extend FlexGen to support\nmulti-GPU settings.\n1We ignore the word embedding layer(s), which is relatively small compared with all the transformer layers.\n4\n\n\n=== PDF page 5 ===\nToken 0\nToken 1\nToken 2\nlayer\nDataset\n(infinite)\n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \nbatch\n(a) Row-by-row schedule\nDataset\n(infinite)\n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \nbatch\nblock\n(b) Zig-zag block schedule\nFigure 3. Two different schedules. The red arrows denote the\ncomputation order.\nfor i = 1 to generation length do\nfor j = 1 to num layers do\n// Compute a block with multiple GPU batches\nfor k = 1 to num GPU batches do\n// Load the weight of the next layer\nload weight(i, j + 1, k)\n// Store the cache and activation of the prev batch\nstore activation(i, j, k −1)\nstore cache(i, j, k −1)\n// Load the cache and activation of the next batch\nload cache(i, j, k + 1)\nload activation(i, j, k + 1)\n// Compute this batch\ncompute(i, j, k)\n// Synchronize all devices\nsynchronize()\nend for\nend for\nend for\nFigure 4. Block schedule with overlapping.\n4.1. Problem Formulation\nConsider a machine with three devices: a GPU, a CPU, and a disk. The GPU and CPU can perform computation while the\ndisk cannot. The three devices form a three-level memory hierarchy where the GPU has the smallest but fastest memory and\nthe disk has the largest but slowest memory. When an LLM cannot ﬁt entirely within the GPU, we need to ofﬂoad it to\nsecondary storage and perform computation part-by-part by partially loading the LLM.\nWe formulate the generative inference with ofﬂoading as a graph traversal problem. Fig. 2 shows an example computational\ngraph, where the model has 4 layers and we generate 3 tokens per prompt. As our focus is throughput-oriented scenarios,\nwe assume a given dataset with an inﬁnite number of prompts that need to be processed. In the ﬁgure, a square means the\ncomputation of a batch on a layer on the GPU. The squares with the same color share the same layer weights. We deﬁne a\nvalid path as a path that traverses (i.e., computes) all squares, while subject to the following constraints:\n• A square can only be computed if all squares to its left on the same row were computed.\n• To compute a square on a device, all its inputs (weights, activations, cache) must be loaded to the same device.\n• After being computed, a square produces two outputs: activations and KV cache. The activations should be stored until\nits right sibling is computed. The KV cache should be stored until the rightmost square on the same row is computed.\n• At any time, the total size of tensors stored on a device cannot exceed its memory capacity.\nThe goal is to ﬁnd a valid path that minimizes the total execution time, which includes the compute cost and I/O cost when\nmoving tensors between devices.\n4.2. Search Space\nGiven the formulation above, we construct a search space for possible valid strategies in FlexGen.\nCompute schedule. Intuitively, there are two orders to traverse the graph in Fig. 2: row-by-row and column-by-column. All\nexisting systems (Aminabadi et al., 2022; HuggingFace, 2022) traverse the graph row-by-row, as shown in Fig. 3(a). This is\nreasonable because it is the fastest way to ﬁnish the generation for one batch and the KV cache can be freed immediately\nafter a row. However, because every two contiguous squares do not share weights, this schedule has to repeatedly load the\nweights and incurs huge I/O costs.\nTo reduce the I/O costs of the weights, we can traverse the graph column-by-column. All squares in a column share weights,\nso we can let the weights stay on GPU for reusing and only load/unload the activations and KV cache. However, we cannot\ntraverse a column all the way to the end because the activations and KV cache still need to be stored. Hence, we have to stop\nwhen they ﬁll the CPU and disk memory. Taking all this into consideration, we converge to a zig-zag block schedule, as\nshown in Fig. 3(b). Besides the zig-zag block schedule, there exists another more advanced and I/O-optimal schedule. We\nonly implement the simpler block schedule due to the practical implementation difﬁculty of the optimal schedule. However,\nwe prove that the block schedule is at most twice worse than the optimal schedule in Appendix A.2.\n5\n\n\n=== PDF page 6 ===\nTheorem 4.1. The I/O complexity of the zig-zag block schedule is within 2× of the optimal solution.\nAnother typical optimization is overlapping. We can overlap the weights load of the next layer, cache/activation load of the\nnext batch, cache/activation store of the previous batch, and the computation of the current batch. Adding overlapping to\nthe block schedule results in Fig. 4. The ﬁrst six functions in the innermost loop can be seen as launched in parallel with\nsix logical threads because there are no dependencies. The last function then synchronizes these six logical threads. We\nrely on operating systems and CUDA drivers to resolve the schedule of the underlying hardware resources. The algorithm\nintroduces two parameters into our search space: the GPU batch size and the number of GPU batches in a block. The\nproduct of the GPU batch size and the number of GPU batches is called block size (or effective batch size).\nTensor placement. Besides compute schedule, a strategy should specify how to store these tensors within the three-level\nmemory hierarchy. We use three variables wg, wc, and wd to deﬁne the percentages of weights stored on GPU, CPU, and\ndisk respectively. Similarly, we use three variables hg, hc, hd to deﬁne the percentages of activations and use cg, cc, cd\nfor the KV cache. Given the percentages, there are still multiple ways to partition the tensors. Taking weight tensors as\nan example, from coarse grain to ﬁne grain, we can partition the weights at the model granularity (e.g., assign 50% of the\nlayers in a model to the GPU), at the layer granularity (e.g., assign 50% of the tensors in a layer to the GPU), or at the tensor\ngranularity (e.g., assign 50% of the elements in a tensor to the GPU). Coarser granularity leads to lower runtime overhead\nbut it is less ﬂexible and its cost is difﬁcult to analyze. Considering both the runtime overhead and desired ﬂexibility, we use\nlayer granularity for weights, and tensor granularity for activations and the KV cache.\nComputation delegation. While CPUs are much slower than GPUs, we ﬁnd using CPU compute can still be beneﬁcial in\nsome cases. This is because the computation of attention scores during decoding is I/O-bounded. Consider a case where the\nKV cache is stored on the CPU. Computing the attention scores on the GPU requires moving the entire KV cache to the\nGPU, which incurs a substantial I/O cost as the KV cache is huge. In contrast, computing the attention score on the CPU\ndoes not require moving the KV cache. It only requires moving the activations from the GPU to the CPU. Quantitatively,\nlet b be the GPU batch size, s be the sequence length, and h1 be the hidden size. The size of the moved KV cache is\nb × s × h1 × 4 bytes, and the size of the moved activation is b × h1 × 4 bytes, so computing attention score on CPU reduces\nI/O by s×. For long sequences (e.g., s ≥512), it is better to compute the attention scores on the CPU if the associated KV\ncache is stored on the CPU or disk.\n4.3. Cost Model and Policy Search\nThe schedule and placement in Section 4.2 constructs a search space with several parameters. Now we develop an analytical\ncost model to estimate the execution time given these algorithm parameters and hardware speciﬁcations.\nCost Model. The cost model predicts the latency during preﬁll for one layer denoted as Tpre, and the averaged latency\nduring decoding for one layer denoted as Tgen in one block. The total latency for computing a block can then be estimated\nas T = Tpre · l + Tgen · (n −1) · l, where l is the number of layers and n is the number of tokens to generate.\nAssuming perfect overlapping, Tpre can be estimated as Tpre = max(ctogp, gtocp, dtocp, ctodp, compp), where ctogp,\ngtocp, dtocp, ctodp, compp denote the latency of read from CPU to GPU, write from GPU to CPU, read from disk to CPU,\nwrite from CPU to disk, computation, respectively, during preﬁll for one layer.\nSimilarly, Tgen can be estimated as Tgen = max(ctogg, gtocg, dtocg, ctodg, compg), with ctogg, gtocg, dtocg, ctodg,\ncompg denoting the latency of read from CPU to GPU, write from GPU to CPU, read from disk to CPU, write from CPU to\ndisk, computation, respectively, during decoding for one layer.\nFor I/O terms like dtocg, it is estimated by summing up the I/O events, which contain weights, activations, and cache reads.\nThe size of FP16 weights for one transformer layer is 8h2\n1 + 4h1 · h2 bytes, with h1 denoting the hidden size, and h2\ndenoting the hidden size of the second MLP layer. Let bls be the block size and s be the prompt length; then the size of\nactivations for one layer is 2 · bls · h1. The size of the KV cache for one layer on average is 4 · bls · (s + n\n2 ) · h1. We have to\nload wd, hd, cd percent of weights, activations, and the KV cache from the disk respectively so that the total latency of disk\nread is dtocg =\n1\ndisk to cpu bandwidth((8h2\n1 + 4h1 · h2) · wd + 4 · bls · (s + n\n2 ) · h1 · cd + 2 · bls · h1 · hd).\nSimilarly for computation terms, we sum up all computation events, including matrix multiplications and batched matrix\nmultiplications on the CPU and the GPU.\nBesides latency estimation, we also estimate the peak memory usage of the GPU, CPU, and disk, and then we add memory\nconstraints. The full cost model is in Appendix A.3.\n6\n\n\n=== PDF page 7 ===\nPolicy Search. A policy includes 11 variables: block size bls, GPU batch size gbs, weight placement wg, wc, wd, activation\nplacement hg, hc, hd, and KV cache placement cg, cc, cd. In practice, the percentage cannot be an arbitrary real number\nbetween 0 and 1, because the tensor cannot be split arbitrarily. However, we relax the percentage variables in the cost model\nto be any real number between 0 and 1 since it is changing gradually. We solve the problem as a two-level optimization\nproblem. We ﬁrst enumerate a few choices of (bls, gbs) tuple. Typically, gbs is a multiple of 4, and bls is less than 20 so there\nare not too many choices. Then with the ﬁxed bls, gbs, ﬁnding the best placement p = (wg, wc, wd, cg, cc, cd, hg, hc, hd)\nbecomes a linear programming problem shown in Eq. (1). The linear programming problem can be solved very quickly\nbecause there are only 9 variables. This formulation can also be ﬂexibly extended to include latency constraints and model\napproximate methods such as compression.\nmin\np\nT/bls\ns.t.\ngpu peak memory\n<\ngpu mem capacity\ncpu peak memory\n<\ncpu mem capacity\ndisk peak memory\n<\ndisk mem capacity\nwg + wc + wd\n=\n1\ncg + cc + cd\n=\n1\nhg + hc + hd\n=\n1\n(1)\nTo use the cost model, we run proﬁling on the hardware to sample some data points and ﬁt the hardware parameters. We\nthen call the optimizer to get an ofﬂoading policy. Due to our relaxation and the hardness of accurately modeling peak\nmemory usage (e.g., fragmentation), sometimes a strategy from the policy search can run out of memory. In this case, we\nmanually adjust the policy slightly. The cost model can usually return a good policy, but it is common that a better policy\ncan be obtained by tuning manually.\n4.4. Extension to Multiple GPUs\nWe discuss how to extend the ofﬂoading strategy in FlexGen if there are multiple GPUs. Although we can ﬁnd a nearly\noptimal strategy for one GPU, the strategy is still heavily limited by I/O and has a low GPU utilization. If we are given more\nGPUs and more CPUs, model parallelism can be utilized to reduce the memory pressure of each GPU, which can potentially\nlead to a super-linear scaling in decoding.\nThere are two kinds of model parallelisms: tensor parallelism and pipeline parallelism (Narayanan et al., 2021; Zheng\net al., 2022). Tensor parallelism can reduce the single-query latency but pipeline parallelism can achieve good scaling on\nthroughput due to its low communication costs. Since we target throughput, FlexGen implements pipeline parallelism.\nWe use pipeline parallelism by equally partitioning an l-layer LLM on m GPUs, and then the execution of all GPUs follows\nthe same pattern. The problem is reduced to running an n/m-layer transformer on one GPU. We can directly reuse the\npolicy search developed for one GPU. To achieve micro-batch pipelining, a new for-loop is added to Fig. 4 to combine the\niteration-level pipeline parallel execution schedule (Huang et al., 2019; Yu et al., 2022) with our single-device ofﬂoading\nruntime.\n5. Approximate Methods\nThe previous section focuses on the exact computation. However, the inference throughput can be greatly boosted with\nnegligible accuracy loss by allowing some approximation, because LLMs are typically robust to careful approximations.\nThis section introduces two such approximations: group-wise quantization and sparse attention.\nGroup-wise Quantization. We show that both the weights and KV cache can be directly quantized into 4-bit integers\nwithout any retraining or calibration on OPT-175B, all while preserving similar accuracy (Section 6.2). When compared to\nsome related works (Yao et al., 2022; Dettmers et al., 2022; Xiao et al., 2022) that try to use integer matrix multiplication\nmainly for accelerated computation, the goal of quantization in our case is primarily for compression and reducing I/O costs.\nTherefore, we can choose a ﬁne-grained quantization format in favor of a high compression ratio and dequantize the tensors\nback to FP16 before computation. We use a ﬁne-grained group-wise asymmetric quantization method (Shen et al., 2020).\nGiven a tensor, we choose g contiguous elements along a certain dimension as a group. For each group, we compute the min\nand max of the group elements and quantize each element x into b-bit integers by xquant = round\n\u0010\nx−min\nmax−min × (2b −1)\n\u0011\n.\n7\n\n\n=== PDF page 8 ===\nTable 1. Hardware Specs\nDevice\nModel\nMemory\nGPU\nNVIDIA T4\n16 GB\nCPU\nIntel Xeon @ 2.00GHz\n208 GB\nDisk\nCloud default SSD (NVMe)\n1.5 TB\nThe tensors are stored in the quantized format and converted back to FP16 before computation. Since both the weights and\nKV cache consume a signiﬁcant amount of memory, we compress both to 4 bits with a group size of 64. There are multiple\nways to choose which dimension to group on. We ﬁnd that grouping the weights along the output channel dimension and the\nKV cache along the hidden dimension preserves the accuracy while being runtime-efﬁcient in practice. One thing to mention\nis that such a ﬁne-grained group-wise quantization in FlexGen causes some overhead in compression and decompression.\nSuch an overhead could be very signiﬁcant if run on a CPU which makes the CPU delegation useless, so we turn off the CPU\ndelegation when enabling quantization. A concurrent work (Dettmers & Zettlemoyer, 2022) also ﬁnds that 4-bit precision\nis almost optimal for total model bits and zero-shot accuracy on OPT models. Compared to this previous work, we ﬁrst\npropose to compress the KV cache and present the results on OPT-175B.\nSparse Attention. We demonstrate that the sparsity of self-attention can be exploited by only loading the top 10% attention\nvalue cache on OPT-175B, all while maintaining the model quality. We present one simple Top-K sparse approximation.\nAfter computing the attention matrices, for each query, we calculate the indices of its Top-K tokens from the K cache. We\nthen simply drop the other tokens and only load a subset of the V cache according to the indices.\nThe application of these approximations is straightforward. We present these preliminary but interesting results and intend\nto emphasize that FlexGen is a general framework that can seamlessly plug in many approximation methods.\n6. Evaluation\nHardware. We run experiments on the NVIDIA T4 GPU instances from Google Cloud. The hardware speciﬁcations are\nlisted in Table 1. The read bandwidth of SSD is about 2GB/s and the write bandwidth is about 1GB/s.\nModel. OPT models (Zhang et al., 2022) with 6.7B to 175B parameters are used in the evaluation. Although we do not\nevaluate other models, the ofﬂoading in FlexGen can be applied to other transformer LLMs, e.g., GPT-3 (Brown et al.,\n2020), PaLM (Chowdhery et al., 2022), and BLOOM (Scao et al., 2022) because they all share a similar structure.\nWorkload. Our focus is high-throughput generation on a given dataset. We use synthetic datasets where all prompts are\npadded to the same length. The system is required to generate 32 tokens for each prompt. We test two prompt lengths: 512\nand 1024 (for experiments in more settings, see Appendix A.4). The evaluation metric is generation throughput, deﬁned as\nthe number of generated tokens / (preﬁll time + decoding time). Sometimes even running a full batch takes too long for\ncertain systems — in this cases, we generate fewer tokens and project the ﬁnal throughput. We use dummy model weights\nin throughput benchmarks for FlexGen and all baselines, but we use real weights for accuracy evaluations.\nBaseline. We use DeepSpeed ZeRO-Inference (Aminabadi et al., 2022) and Hugging Face Accelerate (HuggingFace,\n2022) as baselines. They are the only systems that can run LLMs with ofﬂoading when there is not enough GPU memory.\nDeepSpeed supports ofﬂoading the whole weights to the CPU or disk. It uses ZeRO data parallelism if there are multiple\nGPUs. Accelerate supports ofﬂoading a fraction of the weights. It does not support distributed GPUs on different machines.\nBoth of them use the row-by-row schedule and can only put cache/activations on GPU. These systems support different\nquantization methods. However, the quantization in Accelerate is not compatible with ofﬂoading, and the quantization in\nDeepSpeed cannot preserve accuracy up to 175B, so we do not enable quantization on these systems.\nImplementation. FlexGen is implemented on top of PyTorch (Paszke et al., 2019). FlexGen manages multiple CUDA\nstreams and CPU threads to overlap I/O with compute. FlexGen creates ﬁles for tensors stored on the disk and maps them as\nvirtual memory to access them.\n6.1. Ofﬂoading\nMaximum throughput benchmark. We ﬁrst evaluate the maximum generation throughput the systems can achieve with\none GPU on two prompt lengths. As shown in Table 2, FlexGen outperforms all baselines in all cases. On OPT-6.7B,\nAccelerate and FlexGen can successfully ﬁt the whole model into a single GPU, so they choose to only use the GPU.\nDeepSpeed has a higher memory overhead and cannot ﬁt OPT-6.7B into the GPU, so it uses slower CPU ofﬂoading. DeepSpeed and Accelerate store the KV cache on the GPU, so they cannot\nuse a very large batch size, while FlexGen ofﬂoads most weights and all KV cache to the CPU and enables a larger GPU\nbatch size. Baseline systems can only use a maximum batch size of 2, but FlexGen can use a GPU batch size of 32\nand a block size of 32 × 8, achieving a 69× higher throughput. With compression enabled, FlexGen achieves a 112× higher\ngeneration throughput on a single GPU for prompt sequence length 512. This huge improvement is because FlexGen uses an\neffective batch size of 144 and compresses the weights and KV cache to ﬁt into CPU memory to avoid slow disk swapping.\nMore details on the policy setups and effective batch sizes can be found in Appendix A.4. We also include Petals (Borzunov\net al., 2022) as an additional baseline. Petals uses 1 x T4 for OPT-6.7B, 4 x T4 for OPT-30B, and 24 x T4 for OPT-175B, but\nreports per-GPU throughput. The models are run in INT8 as the default for petals. We benchmark it under a good network\nassumption with a delay of less than 5ms and bandwidth of 1 Gbps. For a more comprehensive comparison with Petals, see\nSection 6.3.\nTable 3 shows the results on 4 machines with one GPU on each machine. Even with 4 GPUs, OPT-30B or OPT-175B still\ncannot ﬁt into these GPUs. Naively, we can run 4 independent FlexGen in a data-parallel fashion to get a linear scaling\non throughput. But here we show that pipeline parallelism can achieve super-linear scaling on decoding throughput. With\npipeline parallelism, the memory pressure of each machine is reduced so we can switch from small batch sizes to larger\nbatch sizes, or switch from disk ofﬂoading to CPU-only ofﬂoading. In Table 3, FlexGen does not achieve linear scaling on\ngeneration throughput (which counts both preﬁll and decoding time costs). This is because there are pipeline bubbles during\nthe preﬁll stage and our workload settings only generate 32 tokens. However, FlexGen achieves super-linear scaling on\ndecoding throughput (which only counts decoding time costs assuming the preﬁll is done). This means if we generate more\ntokens, pipeline parallelism will show its beneﬁts as decoding time will dominate.\nLatency-throughput trade-off. We conﬁgure these systems to achieve maximum throughput under various latency\nconstraints and draw their latency-throughput trade-off curves in Fig. 1. FlexGen sets a new Pareto-optimal frontier that\nsigniﬁcantly outperforms baselines. On the low-latency side, FlexGen supports partial ofﬂoading and uses more space for\nweights. On the high-throughput side, FlexGen aggressively ofﬂoads all things out of the GPU to achieve a large GPU batch\nsize and block size. Given the same latency requirement of 5000 seconds, FlexGen without compression can achieve a 40×\nhigher throughput compared to DeepSpeed and Accelerate. If allowing a higher latency and compression, FlexGen can\nfurther boost throughput and reach a 100× improvement by using an effective batch size of 144. In this case, compression\nenables FlexGen to ﬁt all things in the CPU memory and avoid disk I/O. The detailed latency, throughput, and policy setup\ncan be found in Appendix A.4.\nRuntime breakdown. The detailed\ntask conﬁgurations and running time are in Appendix A.4.\n6.2. Approximations\nWe use two tasks to show that our approximation methods exhibit negligible accuracy loss: next-word prediction on\nLambada (Paperno et al., 2016) and language modeling on WikiText (Merity et al., 2016). As shown in Table 7, “4-bit”\nmeans using group-wise quantization to compress both weights and KV cache into 4-bit integers. “4-bit-S” means combining\nthe quantization and sparse attention with a 10% sparsity on the value cache. Both methods show negligible accuracy\nloss compared to FP16. The results reveal the robustness of LLMs against these approximations. We also tried 3-bit\ncompression but it cannot preserve accuracy.\n6.3. Notations\nWe use notations in Table 8 in this appendix.\nVar\nMeaning\nl\nnumber of layers in the model\ns\nprompt sequence length\nn\noutput sequence length\nbls\nblock size\nh1\nhidden size\nh2\nhidden size of the second MLP layer\nnh\nnumber of head in the model\nTable 8. Notations\nA.2. Compute Schedule Optimality\nThis subsection discusses the graph traversal problem described in Section 4.1 and only considers the case that the model\ncannot ﬁt in a single GPU. We assume no application of CPU computation. To compute a square, the GPU loads the tensors\nit needs and ofﬂoads the cache and activations when ﬁnished. We will analyze two schedules: the zig-zag block schedule\nused in Section 4.2 and an I/O-optimal diagonal block schedule introduced in this section. Note that our analysis only\nconsiders the theoretical I/O complexity. In the real system, the latency and memory consumption cannot be the same as in\nthe theoretical calculations.\nThere are three things that need to be stored during the generation process: weights, activations, and the KV cache. From\nthe computational graph, we have three observations. (1) Suppose we need to swap the weights in and out of the GPU.\nWhatever the portion is, to ﬁnish the generation for one prompt, we need to swap n times for n tokens. Therefore, it would\nbe preferable to reuse the loaded weights for a batch of prompts, amortizing the weights I/O time. (2) Each square will\noutput activations which will be fed into the next layer. Each row in the computational graph only needs to hold activations\nfor one square at the same time. (3) For each square besides the last l squares in a row, the KV cache dumped by the square\ncannot be released until generating the last token (the last l columns in the computational graph). It is not shared across\nrows or columns, which will be the major factor in limiting the batch size.\nA.2.1. ZIG-ZAG BLOCK SCHEDULE AND DIAGONAL BLOCK SCHEDULE\nZig-zag block schedule. Inspired by the three observations introduced in Section 4.2, we compute the ﬁrst column in the\ncomputational graph for bls samples, save the dumped caches and activations, then compute the second column for bls\nsamples, until the last column for bls samples. We call bls as the block size as introduced in Section 4.2. The computed\nbls · n · l squares are called a block.\nAssume FP16 precision, to generate n · bls tokens during one block computation, we have to load n times the whole model\nweights, do I/O operations on activations with 2(2h1 · s · bls · l + 2h1 · bls · l · (n −1)) bytes in total, and do I/O on the KV\ncache with 4h1 · bls · l · (s · n + n(n −1)/2) bytes in total.\nLet w denote the size of one-layer weights. The peak memory used to store the weights, activations, and KV caches can be\nestimated as\npeak mem = w + 2h1 · bls + 4h1 · bls · l · (s + n)\nIf we only swap with CPU, then there is the constraint that peak mem < CPU memory - some overhead. Let cmem denote\nthe right hand, there is\nbls ≤\ncmem −w\n2h1 + 4h1 · l · (s + n) = bls1\nNow we show that there is a better schedule that gives the same I/O efﬁciency but can enlarge the bls by around 2 in some\ncases.\n15\n\n\n=== PDF page 16 ===\nToken 0\nToken 1\nToken 2\nlayer\nDataset\n(infinite)\n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \nbatch\nToken 2\n \n \n \n \nFigure 6. diagonal block schedule\nDiagonal block schedule\nFigure 6 is an illustration of our diagonal block schedule. We have a block containing 4 GPU\nbatches, and we are going to generate 4 tokens with a model that has 4 layers. There will be a one-time warm-up phase\n(gray area) to compute the area above the diagonal. Then for each iteration, the system will compute a diagonal that contains\n4 sub-diagonals (4 squares enclosed by red outlines as the ﬁrst sub-diagonal, then 4 squares enclosed by blue outlines as the\nsecond sub-diagonal). After ﬁnishing the 4 sub-diagonals, it will repeat the same computation in the next row.\nFor simplicity, consider the good case that the memory capacity is large enough that the diagonal can cover all n generation\niterations for n tokens. The block size bls now is deﬁned as the number of samples touched by the diagonal.\nIn total, to compute one diagonal, the weights of each layer will be loaded once, and the I/O of the activations and KV cache\nwill be in size roughly as 1/n as the value in the zig-zag block schedule. There will be bls tokens generated. So the I/O per\ntoken is the same with the zig-zag block schedule after the one-time warm-up if for the same bls.\nThe peak memory needed to hold the necessary weights, activations, and KV cache is estimated as\npeak mem = w + 2h1 · bls + 4h1 · bls · l(2s + n)(n −1)\n2n\nfrom peak mem ≤cmem, we have\nbls ≤\nn(cmem −w)\n2h1 · n + 2h1 · l · (2s + n)(n −1) = bls2\nDespite a one-time warm-up at the beginning. The diagonal block schedule can accommodate a larger block size than\nzig-zag block schedule at the ratio of\nbls2\nbls1\n= 2s + 2n\n2s + n + O\n\u0012 1\nn\n\u0013\nwhich is close to 2 when n ≫s, and close to 1 when s ≫n.\nA larger bls does not change the activations and KV caches I/O per token, but can reduce the weights I/O per token\nproportionally, while weights I/O can normally occupy a large portion.\nDiscussions. In ofﬂoading setting, I/O is a signiﬁcant bottleneck in latency and throughput, so the diagonal block schedule\nshould be able to give considerable gain when n is relatively large compared to s and the memory is sufﬁciently large to ﬁt\nn samples.\nWhen the compute resources are sufﬁcient to avoid ofﬂoading, the diagonal block schedule can still help to reduce the peak\nmemory and enlarge the batch size, which increases GPU utilization.\nAnother beneﬁt compared to the zig-zag block schedule is that with the same throughput, the generation latency for each\nprompt is reduced. For example, suppose in the zig-zag block schedule the bls samples ﬁnish the generation at the same\ntime with latency T. In the diagonal block schedule, the ﬁrst bls/n samples ﬁnish the generation with latency T/n, the\nsecond bls/n samples ﬁnish with latency 2T/n, and so on. The average latency of completion is reduced by half.\nDespite its advantages, there are some difﬁculties in implementing the diagonal block schedule. The major implementation\ndifﬁculty is the dynamic update of the KV cache buffer. To improve runtime efﬁciency, FlexGen now pre-allocates\ncontinuous buffers for all KV cache at the beginning of a block. This works well for the zig-zag block schedule. However,\nfor the diagonal block schedule, pre-allocating continuous buffers make it impossible to save memory anymore. To utilize\n16\n\n\n=== PDF page 17 ===\nthe memory-saving property of the diagonal block schedule, one needs to implement efﬁcient attention computation on\nnon-contiguous memory.\nA.2.2. PROOF OF THEOREM 4.1\nNote that in any case when we move from computing a square to another square, we need to ofﬂoad and load the\ncorresponding KV cache. So that the total I/O incurred by KV cache is constant. The total I/O incurred by activations could\nvary, but despite the preﬁll phase, its size for each square is much smaller than the KV cache for the same square. In total,\nthe size of activations is around 1/(2s + n) of the size of KV cache. We will ignore the I/O incurred by activations for\nsimplicity, which can cause a multiplicative error of 1/(2s + n) at most. Then the only thing left is the weights I/O. Starting\nfrom now, the I/O complexity in the context refers to the I/O complexity incurred by weights.\nDeﬁnition A.1. We deﬁne the working state at any time when the GPU is computing a square as follows. Suppose there\nare k GPU batches working in progress. The column indices of the last squares that have been computed (including the\ncurrent one) are a1, a2, ..., ak, and 1 ≤ai ≤n × l. Different batches are identically independent, so w.l.o.g., suppose\na1 ≥a2 ≥... ≥ak. Then the working state is a tuple (a1, a2, ..., ak). A move that does a computation on a square is a pair\nof states s(1), s(2) that means transit from state s(1) to s(2).\nConsider an optimal order denoted as an inﬁnite sequence m1, m2, ...., m∞, where mi is the ith move. For each i, let si be\nthe current working state.\nLemma A.2. If there is a list of moves that start from state s, and back to state s at the end, the number of computed squares\nfor every column (one layer for one token) is the same.\nProof. Suppose the start state s = (a1, a2, ..., ak). For computations that occupy the whole row, the number of computed\nsquares for every column is the same. So we only need to consider the rows that have not been fully traversed (captured by\nthe end state). For each ai, if the underlying row has not been ﬁnished at the end, and ends with the index bi, then we pair ai\nwith bi. If the underlying row has been ﬁnished, we pair it with a newly opened but not ﬁnished row, still, let bi denote the\nnew index.\nThus we have transited from state Sa = (a1, a2, ..., ak) to another state Sb = (b1, b2, ..., bk). The indices in Sa are sorted\nby a1 ≥a2 ≥... ≥ak. The indices in Sb are not sorted, but bi is paired to ai according to the above paragraph. For each\ni, if bi > ai, we need to count the squares in (ai, bi] by 1. If bi < ai, we need to count the squares in (bi, ai] by -1. Now\nwe argue that for each column index j and 1 ≤j ≤n × l, the count over it is summed to 0. Suppose not, that there are p\npositive count and q negative count and p ̸= q. Then there are p values lower than j in state a and q values lower than j in\nstate b. This contradicts the fact that Sa and Sb are the same state with different orders. Therefore, the number of computed\nsquares for every column is the same.\nTheorem A.3. The diagonal block schedule is I/O-optimal asymptotically.\nProof. Notice that since the memory capacity is ﬁnite, the length of the state is ﬁnite, thus the number of the pos\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 68000:85794]\nhould create the highest possible throughput that pushes from\nstate s to s. Otherwise, we can replace it to get a higher total throughput, which contradicts to that it is an optimal order. So\nthat we can repeat such a strategy between each neighboring ji, ji+1 to get an optimal compute order.\nNow the problem is reduced to ﬁnding an optimal compute order between j1 and j2. With inﬁnite loops, the highest\nthroughput from j1 to j2 gives the highest throughput among the whole sequence.\nAssume an optimal compute order between j1 and j2. From Lemma A.2, there is the same number of squares to be computed\nfor every column denoted as c. With such ﬁxed c, the throughput is determined by the I/O time between j1 and j2. The\nnumber of times we load weights for each color in Figure 2 determines the total I/O time. Each time we load weights, for\nexample, the weights for computing the yellow squares, we cannot compute two yellow squares in the same row without\nother weights swaps, because the squares between them have not been computed and require other weights.\n17\n\n\n=== PDF page 18 ===\nTherefore, for one load, we can only compute squares from different rows, which means all the caches and activations\ncorresponding to those squares need to be held (either on the CPU or on the disk). Every square corresponds to some\nmemory consumption, for example, the squares in the range of the i-th token cost caches for s + i −1 tokens. The sum\nof the memory consumption of all squares is a constant denoted as M. Let M ′ denote the memory capacity. The number\nof weights loading times is at least ⌈M/M ′⌉. Let tw denote the I/O time for loading weights for one color, the optimal\nthroughput is at most c/⌈M/M ′⌉/tw.\nIn the diagonal block schedule, after warm-up, each time with the loaded weights, the peak memory is the sum of the\nmemory consumption of each computed square, which is the same each time we load weights. We can set it to hit M ′2.\nTake c number of diagonals as the repeated list of moves denoted as ⃗q. Set the starting state to be s mentioned before, ⃗q will\nrestore the state to s by construction. The number of weights loading times during ⃗q is ⌈M/M ′⌉, which meets the lower\nbound, and achieves the throughput upper bound c/⌈M/M ′⌉/tw. The warm-up phase can be ignored in the setting of an\ninﬁnite sequence. In summary, the diagonal block schedule is I/O optimal asymptotically.\nThe zig-zag block schedule is not optimal, as the peak memory consumption is not the same each time loading the weights.\nWhen computing the layers for the last token, the peak memory is scaled with s + n −1, while for the ﬁrst token, it is scaled\nwith s. In order to let the former ﬁt in M ′, the latter must be smaller than M ′. But the memory consumption change is\nlinear when generating the tokens, thus the average memory consumption for each weights loading can be pushed to at\nleast M ′/2. From this, the zig-zag block schedule can achieve the throughput at least c/⌈M/(M ′/2)⌉/tw which is 1/2 of\nthe throughput upper bound. In the inﬁnite sequence setting, this means the zig-zag block schedule can achieve an I/O\ncomplexity that is at most 2× optimal. Therefore, we have:\nTheorem 4.1. The I/O complexity of the zig-zag block schedule is within 2× of the optimal solution.\nA.3. Cost Model\nIn this section, we present the full cost model. Note that we use a single variable to represent constants like bandwidth and\nTFLOPS to simplify the formulation below. In real systems, these constants vary according to the total load. We handle such\ndynamics by using piece-wise functions and adding regularization terms. We carefully model the dynamics by depending\nonly on other constants (e.g., hidden size), so the optimization problem remains a linear programming problem with respect\nto policy variables.\nTable 8 and Table 9 give the meaning of constants used in the cost model.\nVar\nMeaning\nctog bdw\nCPU to GPU bandwidth\ngtoc bdw\nGPU to CPU bandwidth\ndtoc bdw\ndisk to CPU bandwidth\nctod bdw\nCPU to disk bandwidth\nmm flops\nGPU ﬂops per second for matrix multiplication\nbmm flops\nGPU ﬂops per second for batched matrix multiplication\ncpu flops\nCPU ﬂops per second\nwg\npercentage of weights on GPU\nwc\npercentage of weights on CPU\nwd\npercentage of weights on disk\ncg\npercentage of KV cache on GPU\ncc\npercentage of KV cache on CPU\ncd\npercentage of KV cache on disk\nhg\npercentage of activations on GPU\nhc\npercentage of activations on CPU\nhd\npercentage of activations on disk\nTable 9.
